Torsion higher Chow cycles modulo ℓ \ell
Theodosis Alexandrou, Lin Zhou

Abstract
. We study the injectivity property of certain actions of higher Chow groups on refined unramified cohomology. As an application for every p ≥ 1 p\geq 1 and for each d ≥ p + 4 d\geq p+4 and n ≥ 2 , n\geq 2, we establish the first examples of smooth complex projective d d -folds X X such that for all p + 3 ≤ c ≤ d − 1 , p+3\leq c\leq d-1, the higher Chow group CH c ⁡ ( X , p ) \operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p) contains infinitely many torsion cycles of order n n that remain linearly independent modulo n n . Our bounds for c c and d d are also optimal. A crucial tool for the proof is morphic cohomology.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
primary 14C15, 14C25, secondary 14F20, 18F25

1.
Introduction

Let
X
be a smooth complex projective variety. It is known that the Chow group of codimension-
i
algebraic cycles on
X
modulo a prime number
\ell
might well be infinite if
2\leq i\leq\dim X-1
. Schoen
[
Schoe02
]
showed that this phenomenon occurs for the triple product of the Fermat elliptic curve and all prime numbers
\ell\equiv 1\mod{3}
. Later, Totaro
[
Tot16
]
building upon earlier works of Rosenschon–Srinivas
[
RS10
]
and of Nori
[
Nor89
]
proved that for a very general principally polarized complex abelian 3-fold
X
, the Chow group
\operatorname{CH}^{2}(X)/\ell
is infinite for all prime numbers
\ell
. An example of infinite-dimensionality of
\operatorname{CH}^{i}(X)/\ell
for all prime numbers
\ell
was also constructed by Diaz
[
Dia21
]
. All the aforementioned results strongly rely on a fundamental theorem of Bloch and Esnault
[
BE96
]
that provides a criterion for a nullhomologous cycle to give a non-zero element in the second Chow group modulo
\ell
. A variant of Bloch and Esnault’s theorem was recently given by Farb–Kisin–Wolfson
[
FKW21
]
, whose proof uses the prismatic theory of Bhatt–Scholze
[
BS22
]
. Scavia
[
Sca24
]
applied their result to extend the main result of
[
Schoe02
]
from
\ell\equiv 1\mod{3}
to all prime numbers
\ell>5
, thus giving the first example of a smooth projective variety
X
over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
such that
\operatorname{CH}^{2}(X)/\ell
is infinite for all but at most finitely many prime numbers
\ell
.

With regards to a problem of Schoen
[
Schoe92
]
, Schreieder
[
Sch24a
]
constructed examples of smooth complex projective
5
-folds with infinite
2
-torsion in their third Griffiths groups, proving so that the torsion subgroup of Griffiths groups is in general not finite. The result was recently extended to odd primes by the first named author
[
Ale23
]
. Since the infinitely many
\ell
-torsion classes considered in
[
Sch24a
,
Ale23
]
are linearly independent modulo
\ell
, one finds that the group
\operatorname{CH}^{3}(X)[\ell]/\ell\operatorname{CH}^{3}(X)[\ell^{2}]
is also infinite.

In the present article, parallel to the above results, we study the structure of Bloch’s higher Chow group
\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p)

[
Blo86
]
. For
p=1
, the
K
-theoretic description
\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,1)\simeq H^{c-1}(X_{\text{Zar}},\mathcal{K}_{c})

[
Qui73
]
allows one to interpret higher cycles in the form
\sum Y_{i}\otimes f_{i},
where
Y_{i}\subset X
are irreducible subvarieties of codimension-
(c-1)
and where
f_{i}\in\mathbb{C}(Y_{i})^{\ast}
are rational functions that respect the rule
\sum\text{div}(f_{i})=0
as a cycle on
X
. It is well-known that the vector space
\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,1)_{\mathbb{Q}}
is in general not countable even modulo decomposable cycles, i.e., modulo the subspace

\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,1)_{\text{dec},\mathbb{Q}}:=\operatorname{im}(\operatorname{CH}^{1}(X,1)\otimes\operatorname{CH}^{c-1}(X)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,1))\otimes\mathbb{Q},

where one recalls that
\operatorname{CH}^{1}(X,1)=\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
. In particular, producing examples with uncountably many indecomposable higher Chow cycles has been the subject of various works; see
[
Col97
,
CF02
,
GL99
,
GL00
,
Lew97
]
.

Interestingly enough, it appears that cases in which the countable groups
\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p)[\ell]
and
\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p)/\ell
are infinite are lacking in the literature. This work is thus dedicated to show the following.

Theorem 1.1
.

For each
p\geq 1
and
n\geq 2
, there is a smooth complex projective variety
X
of dimension
p+4
such that the group
\operatorname{CH}^{p+3}(X,p)[n]/n\operatorname{CH}^{p+3}(X,p)[n^{2}]
contains infinitely many cycles of order
n
.

Considering products
X\times_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{d-(p+4)}_{\mathbb{C}}
for any
d\geq p+4
, the projective bundle formula also gives examples in higher dimensions.

Corollary 1.2
.

Let
p\geq 1
. For each
d\geq p+4
and
n\geq 2,
there is a smooth complex projective
d
-fold
X
such that the group
\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p)[n]/n\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p)[n^{2}]
contains infinitely many cycles of order
n
for all
p+3\leq c\leq d-1
.

We note that the bounds given in the corollary above are optimal. In fact, if either
c<p+2
or
c\geq\dim X
, then the group
\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p)/n
is always finite; see Lemma
2.4
(
1
).
In addition, for each
n\geq 2
, the
n
-torsion subgroup
\operatorname{CH}^{p+2}(X,p)[n]
is finite, since
\operatorname{CH}^{p+2}(X,p)_{\operatorname{tors}}\simeq N^{1}H^{p+3}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(p))
; see Theorem
2.2
(
2
) (or see Lemma
2.4
(
2
)). The latter for
p=0
is a theorem of Merkurjev–Suslin
[
MS83
]
and for
p\geq 1
is a consequence of the Beilinson–Lichtenbaum conjecture proven by Voevodsky
[
Voe03
,
Voe11
]
.

A crucial input for the proof of Theorem
1.1
is the so-called morphic cohomology
L^{c}H^{p}(X)
of
X
. That is, the Poincaré dual of the Lawson homology
L_{\dim(X)-c}H_{2\dim(X)-p}(X)
; see
[
Law87
,
Law89
]
. Lawson laid the foundations for a theory based on the homotopy groups of Chow varieties whose properties have been studied and further developed in various works; see
[
Fri91
,
FL92
,
FM94
,
SV96
,
Fri00
]
. Unlike the case of Bloch’s higher Chow groups that extend the definition of the Chow group of rational equivalence classes of algebraic cycles on a variety
X
, the precursor of the Lawson homology theory replaces the role of rational equivalence by algebraic equivalence. Namely, if
p=2c
, then
L^{c}H^{p}(X)
is identified with the Chow group of codimension-
c
algebraic cycles on
X
modulo algebraic equivalence, i.e.,
L^{c}H^{2c}(X)=\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X)/\sim_{\operatorname{alg}}
. More generally, for an abelian group
A
, the morphic cohomology
L^{c}H^{p}(X,A)
and Bloch’s higher Chow group
\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,2c-p\ ;A)
with coefficients in
A
, are related via a comparison map

\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,2c-p\ ;A)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces L^{c}H^{p}(X,A),

which becomes an isomorphism if one takes
A=\mathbb{Z}/m
; see Theorem
2.8
(
1
). In particular, one finds for all integers
c,p\geq 0
and
n\geq 2,
a natural isomorphism

(1.1)

\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p)[n]/n\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p)[n^{2}]\simeq L^{c}H^{2c-p}(X)[n]/nL^{c}H^{2c-p}(X)[n^{2}];

see Theorem
2.8
(
2
). This allows us to reduce Theorem
1.1
to a statement about morphic cohomology; see Theorem
6.6
.

Our approach towards the proof of Theorem
1.1
is similar to
[
Sch24a
,
Ale23
]
. That is, we establish various injectivity theorems about exterior product maps of higher Chow groups that generalize the results
[
Sch24a
, Theorem 6.1]
and
[
Schoe00
, Theorem 0.2]
; see
\lx@sectionsign
4
. As in
[
Sch24a
]
, these results rely on the fact that several groups of algebraic cycles admit a description in terms of refined unramified cohomology
[
Sch23
]
. Most notably, the kernel of the cycle class map
\bar{\operatorname{cl}}^{p,c}_{X}:\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,\ 2c-p\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})\to H^{p}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n(c))
, which became accessible by recent works of Kok and the second named author, as well as the first named author and Schreieder; see
[
KZ24
,
AS24
]
. The injectivity theorem that is relevant to Theorem
1.1
has the following consequence.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
n\geq 2
be an integer. Let
Y
be a smooth complex projective variety. Then there is a smooth complex projective surface
S
with
\operatorname{Pic}(S)_{\operatorname{tors}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
such that the exterior product map

\displaystyle L^{2}H^{3}(S)/n\otimes L^{c}H^{2c-p}(Y)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces L^{c+2}H^{2c-p+3}(S\times Y)/n,\ [\Gamma]\otimes[z]\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces[\Gamma\times z]

is injective for all integers
c,p\geq 0
.

The condition
\operatorname{Pic}(S)_{\operatorname{tors}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
in the above theorem implies

\displaystyle\frac{L^{2}H^{3}(S)[n]}{nL^{2}H^{3}(S)[n^{2}]}=L^{2}H^{3}(S)/n\simeq\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z};

see Lemma
2.9
(
2
).

Thus, Theorem
1.1
in turn follows from Theorem
1.3
if one uses first the following result and then applies the isomorphism (
1.1
).

Theorem 1.4
.

For each
p\geq 0
, there is a smooth complex projective variety
X
of dimension
p+3
such that the group
L^{p+2}H^{p+4}(X)/n
contains infinitely many elements of order
n
for all integers
n\geq 2
.

The special cases
p\in\{0,1\}
that appear in the above theorem have already been explored; see
[
Tot16
,
Ros08
]
. Moreover, Theorem
1.4
also relies on an injectivity result similar to Theorem
1.3
, where we replace the surface
S
, with a very general elliptic curve
E
; see Corollary
6.3
. Taking products with projective spaces, we obtain a generalization of Theorem
1.4
in higher dimensions.

Corollary 1.5
.

For each
p\geq 0
and
d\geq p+3,
there is a smooth complex projective
d
-fold
X
such that the group
L^{c}H^{2c-p}(X)/n
contains infinitely many elements of order
n
for all
n\geq 2
and all
p+2\leq c\leq d-1
.

The bounds for
d
and
c
given in the above corollary are again optimal for all
p\geq 0
. In fact, we have a canonical isomorphism
\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\simeq L^{c}H^{2c-p}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n)
(see Theorem
2.8
(
1
)) and the former group is known to be finite if either
c\leq p+1
or
c\geq d
; see Lemma
2.4
(
1
).

Besides the case
p=0
, it appears that a variant of Theorem
1.4
(and of Corollary
1.5
) for Bloch’s higher Chow groups is not known if
p\geq 1
. That is, for each
p\geq 1,
a smooth complex projective variety
X
of dimension
p+3
such that
\operatorname{CH}^{p+2}(X,p)/\ell
is infinite for all prime numbers
\ell
. The absence of such a result explains the necessity of working with morphic cohomology in the current work. Furthermore, regarding the case
p=0
in Theorem
1.1
, we are unaware of a single example of a smooth complex projective
4
-fold
X
such that the group
\operatorname{CH}^{3}(X)[\ell]/\ell\operatorname{CH}^{3}(X)[\ell^{2}]
is infinite for some prime number
\ell
. This would be consistent with the expected optimal lower bounds. Note that the same question for
\operatorname{CH}^{3}(X)[\ell]
has an affirmative answer by
[
Schoe00
, Corollary 3.4]
.

As in
[
Ale23
]
the surfaces considered in Theorem
1.3
arise as
\mathbb{Z}/n
-quotients of smooth complete intersections inside
\mathbb{P}^{5}_{\mathbb{C}}
. However, the main difference between them is that they admit different degenerations. In
[
Sch24a
, Theorem 6.1]
, to show that the obstruction to the injectivity of the exterior product map vanishes, the author needed a particular kind of semi-stable degenerations of algebraic surfaces. Roughly speaking, that there are certain order
n
Brauer classes in the geometric generic fibre of the family that extend across the whole family and while the central fibre splits up into several components, the restriction of these lifts to each component is zero. In
[
Sch24a
, Theorem 7.1]
, it was observed that the so-called flower–pot degenerations of the Enriques surfaces
[
Per77
, Proposition 3.3.1(3)]
enjoy such properties. Later, this geometric construction was further generalized in
[
Ale23
,
\lx@sectionsign
4]
.

In our case, the obstruction is given by the torsion subgroup
\operatorname{NS}(S)_{\operatorname{tors}}
of the Néron–Severi group; see Theorem
4.1
. Namely, the surfaces of Theorem
1.3
admit degenerations with the following peculiarities: there exists an étale cyclic cover of degree
n
of
S
whose associated class in
\operatorname{NS}(S)_{\operatorname{tors}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
is a generator and while the covering map extends as an étale cover along the whole family, it becomes trivial over each component of the central fibre; see Theorem
5.2
. Explicit examples of such surfaces include an Enriques surface that degenerates into a rational polyhedron whose dual graph is a triangulation of
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
, i.e., a type III degeneration; see
[
Kul77
,
Mor81
]
. In contrast to
[
Sch24a
]
, we work with degenerations that are not necessarily semi-stable; see Remark
5.4
. Finally, we also note that the construction from
[
Ale23
, Theorem 4.1]
could not be used here, as the universal covers of the surfaces in loc. cit. ramify when specialize in the central fibre.

The paper is organized as follows. After covering the preliminaries in
\lx@sectionsign
2
, we define actions of higher Chow groups on refined unramified cohomology in
\lx@sectionsign
3
. In
\lx@sectionsign
4
, we provide general injectivity results for such actions. In
\lx@sectionsign
5
, we introduce the surfaces that we use to prove Theorem
1.3
. Lastly, in
\lx@sectionsign
6
, we prove our main results, including Theorems
1.1
,
1.3
and
1.4
.

1.1.
Notation

The
n
-torsion subgroup of an abelian group
A
is denoted by
A[n]:=\{a\in A|\ na=0\}
. If
\ell
is a prime number, then
A[\ell^{\infty}]:=\{a\in A|\ \ell^{r}a=0\ \text{for some }r\geq 1\}
is the subgroup of
\ell
-primary torsion elements of
A
. By slight abuse of notation, the cokernel of a homomorphism of abelian groups
\psi:A\to B
will be denoted by
B/A:=\operatorname{coker}(\psi)
.

Let
k
be a field. An algebraic
k
-scheme
X
is a separated scheme of finite type over
k
and
it is equi-dimensional if its irreducible components have all the same dimension. A variety over
k
is an
integral algebraic
k
-scheme. If
k\subset K
is a field extension and
X
is a scheme over
k
, then we also set
X_{K}:=X\times_{\operatorname{Spec}k}\operatorname{Spec}K
.

2.
Preliminaries

We gather some of the important properties of refined unramified cohomology that we need for later use. This also includes the specialization maps constructed in
[
Sch24a
]
. The reader is advised to consult
[
Sch23
,
Sch24b
,
AS24
,
KZ24
]
, from which most of the material we discuss here comes from.

Throughout this article, we work with the following cohomology theories.

2.1.
Continuous étale cohomology

Let
X
be a scheme and let
n\geq 2
be an integer invertible in
X
. We write

(2.1)

\displaystyle H^{i}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n(j)):=R^{i}\Gamma(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mu^{\otimes j}_{n})

for the étale cohomology groups of the sheaf
\mu^{\otimes j}_{n}
; see
[
Mil80
]
. Here, we recall that
\mu_{n}
is the étale sheaf of
n
-th roots of unity and that its twists
\mu^{\otimes j}_{n}
are defined, as follows:

\mu^{\otimes j}_{n}:=\begin{cases}\mu_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mu_{n}\ (j\text{-times}),\ \text{if}\ j\geq 1\\
\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z},\ \text{if}\ j=0\\
\operatorname{Hom}(\mu^{\otimes-j}_{n},\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}),\ \text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

If
\ell
is a prime number, then we also set

(2.2)

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\operatorname{cont}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(j)):=\operatorname{R}^{i}(\Gamma\circ\varprojlim)(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},(\mu_{\ell^{r}}^{\otimes j})_{r}),

for Jannsen’s continuous
\ell
-adic étale cohomology; see
[
Jan88
]
. By slight abuse of notation, we prefer to write

(2.3)

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\operatorname{cont}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{n}(j)):=\bigoplus_{\ell|n}H^{i}_{\operatorname{cont}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(j)),

where
n\geq 2
is again a natural number invertible in
X
.

2.2.
Motivic cohomology

Let
k
be a field and let
X
be a smooth and equi-dimensional algebraic
k
-scheme. We denote by
z^{\ast}(X,\bullet),
the cycle complex of abelian groups introduced by Bloch
[
Blo86
]
, whose homology groups define the higher Chow groups

\operatorname{CH}^{\ast}(X,p):=H_{p}(z^{\ast}(X,\bullet)).

Similarly, if
A
is any abelian group, then
\operatorname{CH}^{\ast}(X,p\ ;A)
are given by the homology groups of
z^{\ast}(X,\bullet)\otimes A
. The complex
z^{\ast}(X,\bullet)
is contavariantly functorial for flat morphisms and thus defines a complex of sheaves
z^{\ast}_{X}(\bullet)_{\tau}
on the small site
X_{\tau},
where
\tau\in\{\text{Zar},\text{\'{e}t}\}
.

We define motivic and Lichtenbaum cohomology of
X
with values in
A
by

(2.4)

\displaystyle H^{p}_{M}(X,A(c)):=H^{p}(X_{\text{Zar}},A_{X}(c)_{\text{Zar}})

and

(2.5)

\displaystyle H^{p}_{L}(X,A(c)):=H^{p}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},A_{X}(c)_{\text{\'{e}t}}),

respectively, where
A_{X}(c):=(z^{c}_{X}(\bullet)_{\text{Zar}}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}A)[-2c]
is the weight
c
motivic complex tensored with
A
. By work of Geisser–Levine
[
GL00
,
GL01
]
, we have canonical quasi-isomorphisms

(2.6)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{Z}/m)_{X}(c)_{\text{\'{e}t}}\simeq\begin{cases}\mu_{m}^{\otimes c}\ \ \ \ &\text{if $m$ is coprime to $\operatorname{char}(k)$;}\\
W_{r}\Omega_{X,\log}^{c}[-c]\ \ \ \ &\text{if $m=q^{r}$ and $q=\operatorname{char}(k)>0$,}\end{cases}

where
W_{r}\Omega_{X,\log}^{c}
is the logarithmic de Rham Witt sheaf defined in
[
Il79
]
.
The first quasi-isomorphism relies on two fundamental results; see
[
GL01
, Theorem 1.5]
. A theorem of Suslin–Voevodsky
[
SV96
, Corollary 7.8]
, which correlates Suslin homology with étale cohomology as well as a result of Suslin
[
Sus00
, Theorem 3.2]
. The latter gives a comparison between the Suslin homology and motivic cohomology. The second quasi-isomorphism (see
[
GL00
, Theorem 8.5]
) follows from a central result of Bloch–Gabber–Kato
[
BK86
]
, which relates Milnor’s
K
-theory and logarithmic de Rham Witt sheaves of
fields. In addition to that, it depends on a comparison result of Nesterenko-Suslin and Totaro between motivic cohomology and Milnor’s
K
-theory; see
[
NS89
,
Tot92
]
.

2.3.
Refined unramified cohomology

In the current work we only consider refined unramified cohomology
[
Sch23
]
for the theories given in
\lx@sectionsign
2.1
and
\lx@sectionsign
2.2
and only for smooth and equi-dimensional algebraic
k
-schemes. This is enough for the main applications.

For a smooth and equi-dimensional algebraic
k
-scheme
X
, we denote by
F_{\ast}X
the increasing filtration given by

F_{0}X\subset F_{1}X\subset F_{2}X\subset\cdots\subset F_{\dim X}X=X,\ \text{where}\ F_{c}X=\{x\in X|\ \operatorname{codim}_{X}(x)\leq c\}.

Above, the codimension of a point
x\in X
is given by

\operatorname{codim}_{X}(x):=\dim X-\dim\overline{\{x\}}.

Alternatively, the sets
F_{c}X
can be viewed as pro-schemes made of open subsets
U\subset X
that contain all codimension
c
points of
X
. We set

(2.7)

\displaystyle H^{\ast}(F_{c}X,A(j)):=\varinjlim_{F_{c}X\subset U\subset X}H^{\ast}(U,A(j)),

where the cohomology functor is any of the
\eqref{eq:cohomology-mu_n}-\eqref{eq:lichtenbaum}
and where for the cases (
2.1
) and (
2.3
) the abelian group
A
equals
\mathbb{Z}/n
and
\mathbb{Z}_{n}:=\bigoplus_{\ell|n}\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
with
n\in k^{\ast}
, respectively. Restriction to open subsets yields a natural map

H^{\ast}(F_{c+1}X,A(j))\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{\ast}(F_{c}X,A(j))

and one defines

\displaystyle H^{\ast}_{c,nr}(X,A(j)):=\operatorname{im}(H^{\ast}(F_{c+1}X,A(j))\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{\ast}(F_{c}X,A(j)))

as the
c
-th refined unramified cohomology of
X
with values in
A(j)
.

By a conjecture of Kok–Zhou
[
KZ24
, Conjecture 1.4]
that was recently proven by the first named author and Schreieder
[
AS24
]
, there are canonical isomorphisms

(2.10)

\displaystyle H^{\ast}_{c,nr}(X,A(j))\simeq H^{\ast}(X_{\text{Zar}},\tau_{\geq\ast-c}\mathcal{K^{\bullet}}),

where

\mathcal{K}^{\bullet}:=\begin{cases}\operatorname{R}\pi_{\ast}\mu^{\otimes j}_{n},\ \text{if}\ A=\mathbb{Z}/n\ \text{with}\ n\in k^{\ast}\ \text{invertible and}\ H^{\ast}(-,A(j))\ \text{is given by}\ \eqref{eq:cohomology-mu_n},\\
\operatorname{R}\pi_{\ast}(\bigoplus_{\ell|n}\operatorname{R}\varprojlim(\mu^{\otimes j}_{\ell^{r}})_{r}),\ \text{if}\ A=\mathbb{Z}_{n}:=\bigoplus_{\ell|n}\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\ \text{with}\ n\in k^{\ast}\ \text{invertible and}\ H^{\ast}(-,A(j))\ \text{is given by}\ \eqref{eq:jannsen-cohomology-any-n},\\
A_{X}(j)_{\text{Zar}},\ \text{if}\ A\ \text{is any abelian group and}\ H^{\ast}(-,A(j))\ \text{is given by}\ \eqref{eq:motivic},\\
\operatorname{R}\pi_{\ast}A_{X}(j)_{\text{\'{e}t}},\ \text{if}\ A\ \text{is any abelian group and}\ H^{\ast}(-,A(j))\ \text{is given by}\ \eqref{eq:lichtenbaum}\end{cases}

and where
\pi:X_{\text{\'{e}t}}\to X_{\text{Zar}}
is the natural map of sites; see
[
AS24
, Theorem 1.2]
. This generalizes the Bloch–Ogus isomorphism for classical unramified cohomology, i.e.,
c=0
; see
[
BO74
]
.

As a consequence of the isomorphism (
2.10
), one obtains for free the contravariant functoriality of the groups (
2.3
); see
[
AS24
, Corollary 1.6]
. That is, if
f:X\to Y
is any map between smooth and equi-dimensional algebraic
k
-schemes, then there is a functorial pullback map

\displaystyle f^{\ast}:H^{i}_{c,nr}(Y,A(j))\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{i}_{c,nr}(X,A(j)).

Similarly, the refined unramified cohomology groups are covariant functorial for proper maps. Namely, if
f:X\to Y
is a proper morphism between smooth and equi-dimensional algebraic
k
-schemes, then there is a natural pushforward map

(2.13)

f_{\ast}:H^{i}(F_{c}X,A(j))\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{i+2r}(F_{c+r}Y,A(j+r))

where
r:=\dim Y-\dim X
; see
[
Sch24a
, Lemma 2.5]
. This respects the filtration
F_{\ast}
and thus gives a pushforward map

\displaystyle f_{\ast}:H^{i}_{c,nr}(X,A(j))\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{i+2r}_{c+r,nr}(Y,A(j+r))

for refined unramified cohomology.

2.4.
Higher Chow cycles modulo
n
and refined unramified cohomology

Various comparison theorems that correlate some of the refined unramified cohomology groups (
2.3
) with algebraic cycle groups are established in
[
Sch23
,
\lx@sectionsign
7]
. This extends to cycles of arbitrary codimension previous results of Bloch–Ogus
[
BO74
]
, Colliot-Thélène–Voisin
[
CTV12
]
, Kahn
[
Kah12
]
, Voisin
[
Voi12
]
and
Ma
[
Ma17
,
Ma22
]
that use classical unramified cohomology.

Let
X
be a smooth and equi-dimensional algebraic
k
-scheme and let
n\geq 2
be an integer invertible in
k
. It was observed in
[
Sch23
, Lemma 7.13]
that if we set
E^{c}_{n}(X):=\ker(\bar{\operatorname{cl}}^{c}_{X}:\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X)/n\to H^{2c}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n(c))),
where
\bar{\operatorname{cl}}^{c}_{X}
is the reduction modulo
n
of the cycle class map
\operatorname{cl}^{c}_{X}:\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X)\to H^{2c}_{\operatorname{cont}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{n}(c)),
then there is a natural isomorphism

E^{c}_{n}(X)\simeq\frac{H^{2c-1}_{c-2,nr}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n(c))}{H^{2c-1}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n(c))}.

It was later noted that the above isomorphism is part of the following long exact sequence

\displaystyle\cdots\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern H^{p}_{p-c-1,nr}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},(\mathbb{Z}/n)_{X}(c)_{\text{\'{e}t}})\overset{\theta}{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern The above was first constructed for quasi-projective

and for invertible

in

, Theorem 4.16]
. This also holds for any smooth

as well as for

with

, as a consequence of the isomorphism (
2.10
) established in
[
AS24
, Theorem 1.2]
; see
[
AS24
, Corollary 1.3]
.

) was also obtained in

The special cases of (
2.4
) that involve classical unramified cohomology in degrees 3 and 4 have previously been noted in
[
Kah96
, Theorem 1.1]
and

[
Kah12
, Remarques 2.10 (2)]
, respectively.

Important for us are the following groups of higher Chow cycles:

\displaystyle E^{c,p}_{n}(X):=\ker(\bar{\operatorname{cl}}^{c,p}_{X}:\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,2c-p)/n\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{p}_{L}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n(c)))

\displaystyle F^{c,p}_{n}(X):=\ker(\tilde{\operatorname{cl}}^{c,p}_{X}:\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,2c-p;\mathbb{Z}/n)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{p}_{L}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n(c)))

\displaystyle G^{c,p}(X):=\ker(\operatorname{cl}^{c,p}_{X}:\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,2c-p)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{p}_{L}(X,\mathbb{Z}(c))).

These are in turn related to refined unramified cohomology using the exact sequences (
2.4
) and (
2.4
). More precisely, we have the following.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
X
be a smooth and equi-dimensional algebraic scheme over a perfect field
k
. For any integer
n\geq 2
, with notations as in (
2.4
)–(
2.4
), there are canonical isomorphisms

(2.38)

\displaystyle F^{c,p}_{n}(X)\simeq\frac{H^{p-1}_{p-c-2,nr}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},(\mathbb{Z}/n)_{X}(c)_{\text{\'{e}t}})}{H^{p-1}_{L}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n(c))}\ \text{and}\ G^{c,p}(X)\simeq\frac{H^{p-2}_{p-c-3,nr}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})_{X}(c)_{\text{\'{e}t}})}{H^{p-1}_{L}(X,\mathbb{Z}(c))},

where the refined unramified terms are defined in (
2.3
). Moreover, there is a natural exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces E^{c,p}_{n}(X)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces F^{c,p}_{n}(X)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces G^{c,p+1}(X)

via which
E^{c,p}_{n}(X)
identifies with the subgroup

\left\{[\alpha]\in\frac{H^{p-1}_{p-c-2,nr}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n(c))}{H^{p-1}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n(c))}\ \Big|\ \alpha\in\operatorname{im}(H^{p}_{L}(X,\mathbb{Z}(c))\overset{\rho}{\to}H^{p-1}_{p-c-2,nr}(X,(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})_{X}(c)_{\text{\'{e}t}}))\right\},

where
\rho
is the composite map

H^{p}_{L}(X,\mathbb{Z}(c))\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{p}_{p-c-2,nr}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}_{X}(c)_{\text{\'{e}t}})\simeq H^{p-1}_{p-c-2,nr}(X,(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})_{X}(c)_{\text{\'{e}t}})

that appears in (
2.4
).

Proof.

The isomorphisms of (
2.38
) follow immediately from the exact sequences (
2.4
) and (
2.4
), respectively; see
[
AS24
, Corollary 1.3, Corollary 1.4]
. For (
2.1
), one considers the following diagram,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{p}_{L}(X,\mathbb{Z}(c))/n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{p}_{L}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n(c))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{p+1}_{L}(X,\mathbb{Z}(c))[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,2c-p)/n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,2c-p\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,2c-p-1)[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\operatorname{cl}}^{p,c}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\operatorname{cl}}^{p,c}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{cl}^{p,c}_{X}}

where we note that the rows are exact. Thus, the snake lemma implies (
2.1
) along with the last claim. The description of the map
\rho
is explained in the proof of
[
AS24
, Corollary 1.4]
.
∎

2.5.
Higher Chow groups modulo
n

We collect some results about the structure of the groups
\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p)/n
and
\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p)[n]
. The following follows from Rost and Voevodsky’s proof of the Beilinson–Lichtenbaum conjecture; see
[
Ros02
,
Voe03
,
Voe11
]
.

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
k
be a perfect field and let
n\geq 2
be an integer. Let
X
be a smooth and equi-dimensional algebraic scheme over
k
. Then the following hold true:

(1)

We have natural isomorphisms
\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\simeq H^{2c-p}_{L}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n(c))
for all
c\leq p
and

\operatorname{CH}^{p+1}(X,p\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\simeq N^{1}H^{p+2}_{L}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n(p+1)).

(2)

Similarly, we have natural isomorphisms
\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p)_{\operatorname{tors}}\simeq H^{2c-p-1}_{L}(X,\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(c))/H^{2c-p-1}_{M}(X,\mathbb{Q}(c))
for all
c\leq p+1
and

\operatorname{CH}^{p+2}(X,p)_{\operatorname{tors}}\simeq N^{1}H^{p+3}_{L}(X,\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(p+2))/H^{p+3}_{M}(X,\mathbb{Q}(p+2)),

where
N^{1}H^{\ast}_{L}(X,A(c)):=\ker(H^{\ast}_{L}(X,A(c))\to H^{\ast}_{L}(F_{0}X,A(c)))
.

Proof.

The claims in (
1
) correspond to the Lichtenbaum–Beilinson Conjecture, the rest are immediate consequences. Away from the characteristic, the conjecture follows from
[
GL01
, Corollary 1.2]
and the Bloch–Kato conjecture proven by Voevodsky
[
Voe11
]
. Moreover, if
n=q^{r}
with
q=\operatorname{char}(k)>0
, then it follows from
[
GL00
, Theorem 8.5]
. We explain how to obtain (
2
). The long exact Bockstein sequence for motivic cohomology gives the exact sequence

H^{2c-p-1}_{M}(X,\mathbb{Q}(c))\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{2c-p-1}_{M}(X,\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(c))\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p)_{\operatorname{tors}}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces 0.

The above middle term identifies with
H^{2c-p-1}_{L}(X,\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(c))
if
c\leq p+1
and with
N^{1}H^{p+3}_{L}(X,\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(p+2))
if
c=p+2
by (
1
). This proves the second claim.
∎

The following is recalled, as we need it for Lemma
2.4
.

Remark 2.3
.

Let
k
be a field and let
n\geq 2
be an integer invertible in
k
. Let
G_{k}:=\operatorname{Gal}(k_{s}/k)
be the absolute Galois group of the field
k
and denote by
M(G_{k})
the abelian category of discrete
G_{k}
-modules. Assume that
G_{k}
has finite cohomology for all finite
n
-torsion
G_{k}
-modules
M
, i.e., the cohomology groups
H^{\ast}(G_{k},M)
defined as the right derived functors of
M(G_{k})\to\operatorname{Ab},N\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces N^{G_{k}}
are finite. Then for any smooth variety
X
over
k
and for all integers
i,j,
the étale cohomology group
H^{i}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n(j))
is finite. In fact, this follows from the finiteness of
H^{i}((X\times_{k}k_{s})_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n(j))
and the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence

E_{2}^{r,s}=H^{r}(G_{k},H^{s}((X\times_{k}k_{s})_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n(j)))\implies H^{r+s}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n(j)).

Note that the assumption made on
G_{k}
is satisfied for separably closed, finite fields and local fields; see
[
Jan88
, Remark (3.5)]
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
k
be a field and let
n\geq 2
be an integer invertible in
k
. Assume that the absolute Galois group
G_{k}:=\operatorname{Gal}(k_{s}/k)
has finite cohomology for all finite
n
-torsion
G_{k}
-modules (see Remark
2.3
) and put
\epsilon:=\operatorname{cd}(k)
for the cohomological dimension of the field
k
. Let
X
be a smooth and equi-dimensional algebraic scheme over
k
of dimension
d
. Then the following hold:

(1)

The group
\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)
is finite if
c\geq d+\epsilon
or
c\leq p+1
. Moreover, we have a natural isomorphism
\operatorname{cl}:\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\simeq H^{2c-p}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n(c))
if
c\geq d+\epsilon
or
c\leq p
.

(2)

The
n
-torsion group
\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p)[n]
is finite if
c\geq d+\epsilon
or
c\leq p+2
.

Proof.

The étale cohomology groups
H^{\ast}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n(c))
are all finite by the assumption made on
G_{k}
; see Remark
2.3
. Thus, the claim (
1
) for
c\leq p+1
follows from Theorem
2.2
(
1
). Next, we consider the long exact sequence (
2.4
), which we rewrite as

\cdots\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{2c-p-1}_{c-p-2,nr}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n(c))\overset{\theta}{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{2c-p}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n(c))\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\cdots.

Recall from (
2.10
) that
H^{2c-p-\ast}_{c-p-\ast-1,nr}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n(c))\simeq H^{2c-p-\ast}(X_{\text{Zar}},\tau_{\geq c+1}\operatorname{R}\pi_{\ast}\mu_{n}^{\otimes c})
. Hence, it suffices to show that if
c\geq d+\epsilon
, then the Zariski complex
\tau_{\geq c+1}\operatorname{R}\pi_{\ast}\mu_{n}^{\otimes c}
is quasi-isomorphic to zero. Indeed, consider the natural injection
\operatorname{R}^{q}\pi_{\ast}\mu_{n}^{\otimes c}\hookrightarrow\bigoplus_{\eta\in X^{(0)}}\iota_{\eta,\ast}H^{q}(k(\eta),\mu_{n}^{\otimes c})
, where
\iota_{\eta,\ast}H^{q}(k(\eta),\mu_{n}^{\otimes c})
is the constant sheaf supported on the component whose generic point is
\eta
; see
[
BO74
, Theorem 4.2]
. Since the group
H^{q}(k(\eta),\mu_{n}^{\otimes c})
is zero for
q>d+\epsilon
, we in turn obtain that
\operatorname{R}^{q}\pi_{\ast}\mu_{n}^{\otimes c}=0
for all
q\geq c+1
, as we wanted; see
[
Stacks24
,
Tag 0F0V
]
. This completes the proof of (
1
).

We now prove (
2
) using (
1
). Once again, the long exact Bockstein sequence for motivic cohomology gives the exact sequence,

H^{2c-p-1}_{M}(X,\mathbb{Z}(c))\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{2c-p-1}_{M}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n(c))\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p)[n]\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces 0.

We note that the middle term of the above sequence identifies with
H^{2c-p-1}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n(c))
if
c\geq d+\epsilon
or
c\leq p+1
by (
1
). Furthermore, if
c=p+2
, then it is isomorphic to
N^{1}H^{p+3}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n(p+2))
; see Theorem
2.2
(
1
). Hence, the group
\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p)[n]
is finite if
c\geq d+\epsilon
or
c\leq p+2
, since the étale cohomology groups
H^{\ast}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n(c))
are finite. This finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Remark 2.5
.

As an application of Lemma
2.4
, we find that for a separably closed field
k,
the group
\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p)/n
is finite if
c\geq d
or
c\leq p+1,
whereas
\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p)[n]
is finite if
c\geq d
or
c\leq p+2
.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
k
be a separable closed field and let
n\geq 2
be an integer invertible in
k
. Let
r\geq 1
be an integer. Let
X
be a smooth, proper and equi-dimensional algebraic scheme over
k
of dimension
d
. Then the canonical maps

\begin{split}&\frac{\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1)[n^{r}]}{n\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1)[n^{r+1}]}\overset{\operatorname{cl}}{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\frac{H^{2d-1}_{\operatorname{cont}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{n}(d))[n^{r}]}{nH^{2d-1}_{\operatorname{cont}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{n}(d))[n^{r+1}]}\\
&\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1)_{\operatorname{tors}}/n\overset{\operatorname{cl}}{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}H^{2d-1}_{\operatorname{cont}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{n}(d))_{\operatorname{tors}}/n\end{split}

are isomorphisms. Moreover, the inclusion
\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1)_{\operatorname{tors}}/n\ \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1)/n
is an equality.

Proof.

First note that we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,2\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n^{r})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1)[n^{r}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2d-2}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n^{r}(d))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2d-1}_{\operatorname{cont}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{n}(d))[n^{r}]},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{cl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{cl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

where the former vertical map is an isomorphism by Lemma
2.4
(
1
). This in turn implies the surjectivity of the latter, as the Bockstein maps
\delta
are also onto. Since we know by the same lemma that the cycle map
\operatorname{cl}:\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\to H^{2d-1}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n(d))
is an isomorphism, we find that, in fact,
\operatorname{cl}:\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1)/n\to H^{2d-1}_{\operatorname{cont}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{n}(d))/n
is injective. Therefore, both observations imply that the maps

\frac{\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1)[n^{r}]}{n\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1)[n^{r+1}]}\overset{\operatorname{cl}}{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\frac{H^{2d-1}_{\operatorname{cont}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{n}(d))[n^{r}]}{nH^{2d-1}_{\operatorname{cont}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{n}(d))[n^{r+1}]}

and
\operatorname{cl}:\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1)_{\operatorname{tors}}/n\to H^{2d-1}_{\operatorname{cont}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{n}(d))_{\operatorname{tors}}/n
are isomorphisms, as claimed.

Finally, we show that the inclusion
\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1)_{\operatorname{tors}}/n\ \hookrightarrow\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1)/n
is an equality. It suffices to show that these finite groups have the same order. To see this, consider the short exact sequence

0\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1)/n\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X)[n]\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces 0

and recall that
\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\simeq H^{2d-1}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n(d))=H^{2d-1}_{\operatorname{cont}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{n}(d))/n.
By Roitman’s theorem
[
Roj80
,
Blo79
]
, the group
\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X)[n]
identifies with the
n
-torsion subgroup
\operatorname{Alb}(X)[n]\simeq(\mathbb{Z}/n)^{b_{1}}
of the Albanese variety of
X
, where
b_{1}
is the first Betti number of
X
; see
[
CTS21
, Remark 5.2.7]
. Hence, the short exact sequence splits, giving so that

|\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1)/n|=|H^{2d-1}_{\operatorname{cont}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{n}(d))/n|/n^{b_{1}}=|H^{2d-1}_{\operatorname{cont}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{n}(d))_{\operatorname{tors}}/n|.

Note that for the last equality we used that the torsion free part of
H^{2d-1}_{\operatorname{cont}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{n}(d))
has rank
b_{1}
by Poincaré duality; see
[
Mil86
, Theorem 1.13]
. Now, the first claim clearly implies the second. The proof is complete.
∎

Corollary 2.7
.

Let
k
be a separable closed field and let
n\geq 2
be an integrer invertible in
k
. Let
X
be a smooth, proper and equi-dimensional algebraic scheme over
k
of dimension
d
. Then there is a natural isomorphism of finite abelian groups

(2.45)

\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1)/n\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{NS}(X)[n],\bigoplus_{\ell|n}\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}/\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}),

where
\operatorname{NS}(X)
is the Néron–Severi group of
X
.

Proof.

We can reduce to the case
n=\ell^{r}
for some prime number
\ell
and positive integer
r
. It is then enough to construct a natural isomorphism
H^{2d-1}_{\operatorname{cont}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(d))[\ell^{\infty}]/\ell^{r}\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{NS}(X)[\ell^{r}],\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}/\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
; see Lemma
2.6
. The Poincaré duality theorem
[
Mil80
, Chapter VI, Corollary 11.2]
for
X
gives a canonical isomorphism

H^{2d-2}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{r}(d))\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(H^{2}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{r}),\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{r}).

This in turn is compatible with respect to the natural inclusion
\mu_{\ell^{r}}^{\otimes d}\hookrightarrow\mu_{\ell^{r+1}}^{\otimes d}
in the first argument, the natural surjection
\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{r+1}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-1.99997pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{r}
in the second argument and the natural inclusion
\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{r}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{r+1}
in the third. Thus, passing to the limit we obtain a canonical isomorphism

(2.46)

H^{2d-2}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}/\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(d))\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(H^{2}_{\operatorname{cont}}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}),\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}/\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}).

Now, taking the quotient of these groups by their maximal divisible subgroups, (
2.46
) yields an isomorphism

(2.47)

H^{2d-1}_{\operatorname{cont}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(d))[\ell^{\infty}]\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{NS}(X)[\ell^{\infty}],\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}/\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}),

where here, we use that
\operatorname{cl}^{1}_{X}:\operatorname{NS}(X)[\ell^{\infty}]\to H^{2}_{\operatorname{cont}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})[\ell^{\infty}]
is an isomorphism; see
[
CTS21
, (5.13)]
. To prove the claim, it remains to show that
\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{NS}(X)[\ell^{\infty}],\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}/\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})\otimes\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{r}\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{NS}(X)[\ell^{r}],\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}/\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}).
The latter follows if one considers the exact sequence

0\to\operatorname{NS}(X)[\ell^{r}]\to\operatorname{NS}(X)[\ell^{\infty}]\overset{\times\ell^{r}}{\to}\operatorname{NS}(X)[\ell^{\infty}]

and then applies the exact functor
\operatorname{Hom}(-,\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}/\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
. The proof is complete.
∎

We close this section with some comparison results between morphic cohomology and Bloch’s higher Chow groups. We recommend
[
Fri91
,
FL92
,
FM94
,
SV96
,
Fri00
]
for readers interested in morphic cohomology or Lawson homology theory.

Theorem 2.8
.

Let
X
be a smooth complex variety. Then for all
c,p\geq 0
and for all
n\geq 2
, there are canonical isomorphisms:

(1)

\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\simeq L^{c}H^{2c-p}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n)
and

(2)

\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p)[n]/n\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p)[n^{2}]\simeq L^{c}H^{2c-p}(X)[n]/nL^{c}H^{2c-p}(X)[n^{2}].

Proof.

The first isomorphism (
1
) is a theorem of Suslin–Voevodsky; see
[
SV96
, Theorem 9.1]
. The latter isomorphism (
2
) is a consequence of the former. Namely, one considers the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p+1\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p)[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L^{c}H^{2c-p-1}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L^{c}H^{2c-p}(X)[n]},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

where the two Bockstein maps
\delta
are surjective and where the former vertical map is an isomorphism by (
1
). Thus, the latter vertical map is onto. Again, using the isomorphism in (
1
), we find that the canonical map
\operatorname{CH}^{c}(X,p)/n\to L^{c}H^{2c-p}(X)/n
is injective. Hence, all together, they imply the isomorphism in (
2
), as we wanted. The proof of Theorem
2.8
is complete.
∎

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
X
be a smooth complex projective variety of dimension
d
such that
\operatorname{NS}(X)_{\operatorname{tors}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}/n
for some
n\geq 2
. Then the following hold:

(1)

We have an equality
\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1)[n]/n\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1)[n^{2}]=\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1)/n
and an isomorphism

\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1)/n\simeq\mathbb{Z}/n.

(2)

Moreover, if
\operatorname{Pic}(X)=\operatorname{NS}(X)
, then we have an equality
L^{d}H^{2d-1}(X)[n]/nL^{d}H^{2d-1}(X)[n^{2}]=L^{d}H^{2d-1}(X)/n
and a canonical isomorphism

L^{d}H^{2d-1}(X)/n\simeq\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1)/n\simeq\mathbb{Z}/n.

Proof.

We note that (
2
) is a consequence of (
1
). Indeed, the Bockstein sequence for morphic cohomology gives a natural isomorphism
L^{d}H^{2d-1}(X)/n\simeq L^{d}H^{2d-1}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n)
, since
L^{d}H^{2d}(X)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
is torsion-free. On the other hand, the condition
\operatorname{Pic}(X)=\operatorname{NS}(X)
implies that the Albanese variety of
X
is trivial and we in turn find from Roitman’s theorem that the torsion in the Chow group of
0
-cycles
\operatorname{CH}_{0}(X)_{\operatorname{tors}}
is zero as well; see
[
Roj80
,
Blo79
]
. Thus, the Bockstein sequence for motivic cohomology yields an isomorphism
\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1)/n\simeq\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)
and we see that (
1
) implies (
2
); see Theorem
2.8
.

Finally, we show (
1
). The condition
\operatorname{NS}(X)_{\operatorname{tors}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}/n
implies that
\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1)/n\simeq\mathbb{Z}/n
; see Corollary
2.7
. Recall that the cycle map
\operatorname{cl}^{1}_{X}:\operatorname{NS}(X)\to H^{2}_{\operatorname{sing}}(X,\mathbb{Z})
yields an isomorphism on the torsion subgroups. Moreover, we see that the torsion in
H^{2}_{\operatorname{sing}}(X,\mathbb{Z})
is the same with the torsion in the singular homology group
H_{1}(X,\mathbb{Z})
by the universal coefficient theorem. Thus, Poincaré duality in turn gives an isomorphism
H^{2d-1}(X,\mathbb{Z})_{\operatorname{tors}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}/n
. Now, the claim (
1
) clearly follows from Lemma
2.6
together with a comparison theorem between continuous
\ell
-adic étale cohomology and singular cohomology; cf.
[
Mil80
, Chapter III, Theorem 3.12]
. The proof of Lemma
2.9
is complete.
∎

2.6.
Specialization map

In this section we recall the construction of the specialization map from
[
Sch24a
,
\lx@sectionsign 4
]
and collect some of its properties that we need.

Let
k
be a field, and let
R=\mathcal{O}_{C,x_{0}}
be the local ring of a smooth
k
-curve
C
at a closed point
x_{0}\in C
. This is a discrete valuation ring, and we fix a uniformizer
\pi\in R
. We also set
K=\operatorname{Frac}(R)
for its fraction field. Let
p:\mathcal{X}\to\operatorname{Spec}R
be a smooth family whose central fibre
X_{0}:=\mathcal{X}\times_{R}k
and generic fibre
X_{\eta}:=\mathcal{X}\times_{R}K
are both equi-dimensional.

We first recall the construction of the specialization map
sp
for étale cohomology with values in
\mathbb{Z}/n
and
n\in k^{\ast}
.

2.6.1.
Construction of
sp
for cohomology

The Gysin exact sequence
[
CTS21
,
\lx@sectionsign 2.3.2
]
for the embedding
X_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}
is given by

(2.48)

\cdots\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{i}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{Z}/n(j))\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{i}(X_{\eta},\mathbb{Z}/n(j))\overset{\partial}{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}H^{i-1}(X_{0},\mathbb{Z}/n(j-1))\overset{\iota_{\ast}}{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\cdots

and we refer to its co-boundary

(2.49)

\partial:H^{i}(X_{\eta},\mathbb{Z}/n(j))\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{i-1}(X_{0},\mathbb{Z}/n(j-1))

as the residue map.

We then define the specialization map with respect to the choice of the uniformizer
\pi\in R
by the formula

(2.50)

sp^{\pi}:H^{i}(X_{\eta},\mathbb{Z}/n(j))\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{i}(X_{0},\mathbb{Z}/n(j)),\alpha\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces-\partial(\alpha\cup(\pi)),

where we view
\pi
as an element of
H^{1}(K,\mathbb{Z}/n(1))=K^{\ast}/{K^{\ast}}^{n}
and keep the notation
(\pi):=p^{\ast}\pi\in H^{1}(X_{\eta},\mathbb{Z}/n(1))
for its pullback; see
[
Sch24a
,
\lx@sectionsign
4.1]
.

Remark 2.10
.

If
k
is algebraically closed, then
sp^{\pi}
no longer depends on
\pi
and so we simply write
sp
, instead. In fact, if
\pi^{\prime}\in R
is another uniformizer, then
u:=\pi^{\prime}\pi^{-1}\in R^{\ast}
is a unit. It follows that
sp^{\pi^{\prime}}-sp^{\pi}=-(\bar{u})\cup\partial,
where
\bar{u}\in H^{1}(R/\pi,\mathbb{Z}/n(1))
denotes the image of
u
in the residue field
R/\pi
; cf. Lemma
2.11
. Thus, if
k
is algebraically closed, then
k=R/\pi
and
H^{1}(k,\mathbb{Z}/n(1))=k^{\ast}/{k^{\ast}}^{n}=0,
which in turn implies
sp^{\pi^{\prime}}-sp^{\pi}=-(\bar{u})\cup\partial=0
.

The following lemma is useful.

Lemma 2.11
.

With notations as above, if
\alpha\in H^{p}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{Z}/n(m))
, then for every
\beta\in H^{i}(X_{\eta},\mathbb{Z}/n(j)),
we have that

sp^{\pi}(\alpha|_{X_{\eta}}\cup\beta)=\alpha|_{X_{0}}\cup sp^{\pi}(\beta).

In particular,
sp^{\pi}(\alpha|_{X_{\eta}})=\alpha|_{X_{0}}
.

Proof.

The last claim follows from the first if one puts
\beta=1\in H^{0}(X_{\eta},\mathbb{Z}/n)=\mathbb{Z}/n
and note that
\partial(\pi)=-1\in H^{0}(X_{0},\mathbb{Z}/n)=\mathbb{Z}/n
; see
[
Sch24a
, Lemma 4.4]
. The first claim follows from
[
Sch21
, Lemma 2.4]
.∎

Remark 2.12
.

The definition (
2.50
) should be compared with Rost’s definition of specialization maps for cycle modules; see
[
Ros96
, pp. 328]
.

2.6.2.
Construction of
sp
for refined unramified cohomology

The analogous construction can be carried out for the étale cohomology of the filtration
F_{\ast}
(
2.7
) with values in
A=\mathbb{Z}/n
. First, since the cup product is compatible with the restriction to open subsets, we obtain well defined maps

(2.52)

\cdots\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise
