0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces 0,

which is induced from the long exact coefficient sequence also known as Bockstein sequence.

Up to the above clarification, the proof of Theorem
4.3
is identical with the one of Theorem
4.1
.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

As explained in Remark
4.2
, the properties

(
P’1
)

and

(
P’2
)

in Theorem
4.3
can also be relaxed if one fixes the free
\mathbb{Z}/n
-submodule
M:=\bigoplus_{\nu=1}^{N}\mathbb{Z}/n\cdot z_{\nu}\subset\operatorname{CH}^{d}(X,1\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)
. That is, for

(
P’1
)

, it is enough to require that

\overline{\operatorname{cl}_{X}(z_{\nu})}\in\operatorname{im}(H^{1}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{Z}/n)\to H^{1}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n)),

where
\overline{\operatorname{cl}_{X}(z_{\nu})}\in H^{1}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n)
is a Poincaré dual of the class
\operatorname{cl}_{X}(z_{\nu})\in H^{2d-1}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n(d))
; see Claim
1
. Moreover, for

(
P’2
)

, it suffices to require that one can choose the lifts of
\overline{\operatorname{cl}_{X}(z_{\nu})}
in
H^{1}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{Z}/n),
in such a way that they become zero when restricted to the components
X_{0i}
of the central fibre
X_{0}:=\mathcal{X}\times_{R}k
.

Remark 4.5
.

Recall that if
X
is any scheme and
G
is a finite group, then the étale cohomology group
H^{1}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},G)
classifies Galois coverings of
X
with group
G
; see
[
Mil80
, Chapter III,
\lx@sectionsign
4]
. Thus, the condition

(
P’1
)

says that
\mathbb{Z}/n
-Galois coverings of
X
lift as
\mathbb{Z}/n
-Galois coverings across the total space
\mathcal{X}
and

(
P’2
)

in turn implies that these coverings become trivial over the components
X_{0i}
of the central fibre
X_{0}
.

5.
Construction

In this section we introduce the surfaces together with their degenerations which will ultimately satisfy the properties

(
P1
)

and

(
P2
)

of Theorem
4.1
. The surfaces we consider have already seen applications in
[
Ale23
]
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field. Let
n\geq 2
be an integer invertible in
k
and let
\zeta\in k
be a primitive
n
-th root of unity. As in
[
Ale23
,
\lx@sectionsign
3]
, we consider the automorphism
\varphi
of
\mathbb{P}^{5}_{k}

(5.1)

(x_{0}:x_{1}:x_{2}:x_{3}:x_{4}:x_{5})\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(x_{0}:\zeta x_{1}:x_{2}:\zeta x_{3}:x_{4}:\zeta x_{5})

whose fixed locus
\operatorname{Fix}(\varphi)
is the union of the two planes

P_{1}:=\{x_{0}=x_{2}=x_{4}=0\}\ \text{and}\ P_{2}:=\{x_{1}=x_{3}=x_{5}=0\}.

We let
\varphi
act on the linear system
|\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{5}_{k}}(n)|
which parameterizes hypersurfaces of
\mathbb{P}^{5}_{k}
of degree
n
and then consider its sublinear system
|\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{5}_{k}}(n)|^{\varphi}
which is defined as the eigenspace of the eigenvalue
\lambda=1
. This in turn is a base-point-free linear system of dimension
d=(n+1)(n+2)-1
.

For general choices of invariant hypersurfaces
H_{i}\in|\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{5}_{k}}(n)|^{\varphi},
with
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, the complete intersection
T:=H_{1}\cap H_{2}\cap H_{3}
is smooth and disjoint from
\operatorname{Fix}(\varphi)
. Hence, the quotient
S:=T/\varphi
is also a smooth projective surface. Note that the first Betti number
b_{1}:=\dim_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}H^{1}_{\operatorname{cont}}(S,\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}(0))
is zero, where
\ell
is any prime invertible in
k
and
\operatorname{Pic}(S)_{\operatorname{tors}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}/n
. Moreover, if
n=2
, then
S
is an Enriques surface and
T
is its canonical double cover, that is, a
K3
surface, while if
n\geq 3,
then
S
is a surface of general type; see
[
Ale23
, Proposition 3.1]
.

We include some of the properties of the surface
S
which will be recalled later.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field and let
n\geq 2
be an integer invertible in
k
. Let
T\subset\mathbb{P}^{5}_{k}
be a smooth complete intersection of three invariant hypersurfaces
H_{i}\in{|\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{5}_{k}}(n)|}^{\varphi}
, such that the automorphism
\varphi
(
5.1
) acts freely on
Y
. Let
S:=T/\varphi
be the corresponding
\mathbb{Z}/n
-quotient of
T
. Then the following assertions are true:

(1)

We have
\operatorname{Pic}(S)=\operatorname{NS}(S)
and
\operatorname{NS}(S)_{\operatorname{tors}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}/n
.

(2)

The Bockstein map yields an isomorphism
\delta:H^{1}(S_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n)\simeq H^{2}_{\operatorname{cont}}(S,\mathbb{Z}_{n})_{\operatorname{tors}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}/n
.

(3)

We have
\operatorname{CH}^{2}(S,1)[n]/n\operatorname{CH}^{2}(S,1)[n^{2}]=\operatorname{CH}^{2}(S,1)/n
and
\operatorname{CH}^{2}(S,1)/n\simeq\mathbb{Z}/n
.

(4)

In addition, if
k=\mathbb{C},
then the Hodge numbers of
S
are given by
h^{2,0}=\frac{n^{2}}{4}(5n^{2}-18n+17)-1
,
h^{1,1}=\frac{n^{2}}{2}(7n^{2}-18n+13),
and
h^{1,0}=0
.

Proof.

The properties are well-known. We include a proof for the sake of completeness. The Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence associated to the
\mathbb{Z}/n
-Galois covering
T\to S,

E^{p,q}_{2}:=H^{p}(\mathbb{Z}/n,H^{q}(T_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{G}_{m}))\implies H^{p+q}(S_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{G}_{m})

yields a short exact sequence

0\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathbb{Z}/n,H^{0}(T_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{G}_{m}))\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\operatorname{Pic}(S)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\operatorname{Pic}(T)^{\mathbb{Z}/n}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces 0,

where we implicitly use the canonical isomorphism
H^{1}(-_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{G}_{m})\simeq\operatorname{Pic}(-)
; see
[
Mil80
, Chapter III, Proposition 4.9]
. The first term of the above sequence is identified with the character group of
\mathbb{Z}/n
and is thus again isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/n
. Now, the claim in (
1
) follows from the above short exact sequence along with the well-known fact that any smooth complete intersection of hypersurfaces in a projective space has invariants
\operatorname{Pic}^{0}
and
\operatorname{Pic}_{\operatorname{tors}}
zero.

Note that the claim in (
2
) is a direct consequence of (
1
). In fact, we have a short exact sequence

0\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{1}_{\operatorname{cont}}(S,\mathbb{Z}_{n})/n\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{1}(S_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{2}_{\operatorname{cont}}(S,\mathbb{Z}_{n})_{\operatorname{tors}}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces 0,

which arises from the long exact Bockstein sequence. The Kummer exact sequence
[
CTS21
,
\lx@sectionsign
3.2.1]
gives an isomorphism
H^{1}(S_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/m)\simeq\operatorname{Pic}(S)[m]
for any invertible integer
m\in k^{\ast}
and hence (
1
) implies
H^{1}_{\operatorname{cont}}(S,\mathbb{Z}_{n})=0
; see
[
Jan88
, (0.2)]
. This proves the claim in (
2
).

Next, we show the claim in (
3
). Note that
H^{3}_{\operatorname{cont}}(S,\mathbb{Z}_{n}(2))\simeq\mathbb{Z}/n
, and thus we find that the cycle class map

\frac{\operatorname{CH}^{2}(S,1)[n]}{n\operatorname{CH}^{2}(S,1)[n^{2}]}\overset{\operatorname{cl}}{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}H^{3}_{\operatorname{cont}}(S,\mathbb{Z}_{n}(2))_{\operatorname{tors}}/n

is an isomorphism; see Lemma
2.6
. Moreover, the same lemma implies

\frac{\operatorname{CH}^{2}(S,1)[n]}{n\operatorname{CH}^{2}(S,1)[n^{2}]}=\operatorname{CH}^{2}(S,1)_{\operatorname{tors}}/n=\operatorname{CH}^{2}(S,1)/n,

as we wanted.

Finally, the claim in (
4
) is contained in
[
Ale23
, Proposition 3.1]
. The proof of the proposition is complete.
∎

The following result gives the desired degeneration of the surface
S
.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field and let
K
be an algebraic closure of the field
k(t)
. Let
n\geq 2
be an integer invertible in
k
. Then there exists a projective flat family
\mathcal{S}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k},0},
such that

(1)

The geometric generic fibre
S:=\mathcal{S}\times_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k},0}}K
is a quotient of the form
T/\varphi,
where
T\subset\mathbb{P}^{5}_{K}
is a smooth
complete intersection of three very general invariant hypersurfaces
H_{i}\in|\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{5}_{K}}(n)|^{\varphi}
and
\varphi
is
the automorphism (
5.1
).

(2)

The central fibre
S_{0}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n^{2}}S_{0i}
is reduced and consists of
n^{2}
rational components
S_{0i}
, each isomorphic to the projective plane
\mathbb{P}^{2}_{k}
. Moreover, the double curves
C_{i,j}:=S_{0i}\cap S_{0j}
sit as projective lines inside each plane
S_{0i}
.

(3)

The
\mathbb{Z}/n
-Galois covering
T\to S
extends to a
\mathbb{Z}/n
-Galois covering
\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{S}
over
\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k},0}
, which in turn is trivial over each component
S_{0i}
of the central fibre
S_{0}
.

Proof.

We first construct the family
\mathcal{T}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k},0}
mentioned in (
3
). For a hyperplane
L\in|\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{5}_{k}}(1)|
we put
L_{\varphi^{i}}:={(\varphi^{i})}^{\ast}(L)\subset\mathbb{P}^{5}_{k}
. We shall use the following lemma.

Lemma 5.3
.

For general
L^{1},L^{2},L^{3}\in|\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{5}_{k}}(1)|
one has that the intersections
L^{1}_{\varphi^{i_{1}}}\cap L^{2}_{\varphi^{i_{2}}}\cap L^{3}_{\varphi^{i_{3}}}
and
L^{1}_{\varphi^{j_{1}}}\cap L^{2}_{\varphi^{j_{2}}}\cap L^{3}_{\varphi^{j_{3}}}
are disjoint if
i_{r}\neq j_{r}
for all
r\in\{1,2,3\},
where
0\leq i_{r},j_{r}\leq n-1
.

Proof of Lemma
5.3
.

This can be translated into a determinant criterion that yields an open condition. We include some of the details. Write

\begin{split}&L^{1}:=\{\alpha_{0}x_{0}+\alpha_{1}x_{1}+\alpha_{2}x_{2}+\alpha_{3}x_{3}+\alpha_{4}x_{4}+\alpha_{5}x_{5}=0\}\\
&L^{2}:=\{\beta_{0}x_{0}+\beta_{1}x_{1}+\beta_{2}x_{2}+\beta_{3}x_{3}+\beta_{4}x_{4}+\beta_{5}x_{5}=0\}\\
&L^{3}:=\{\gamma_{0}x_{0}+\gamma_{1}x_{1}+\gamma_{2}x_{2}+\gamma_{3}x_{3}+\gamma_{4}x_{4}+\gamma_{5}x_{5}=0\}\end{split}

for the equations of
L^{i}
with
\alpha_{r},\beta_{r},\gamma_{r}\in k
. We let
A\in M(6\times 6,k)
be the matrix whose first 3 rows are given by the coefficients of the equations of
L^{r}_{\varphi^{i_{r}}}
with
r\in\{1,2,3\}
and whose last 3 rows are given by the coefficients of the equations of
L^{r}_{\varphi^{j_{r}}}
with
r\in\{1,2,3\}
, that is,

A:=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{0}&\zeta^{i_{1}}\alpha_{1}&\alpha_{2}&\zeta^{i_{1}}\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{4}&\zeta^{i_{1}}\alpha_{5}\\
\beta_{0}&\zeta^{i_{2}}\beta_{1}&\beta_{2}&\zeta^{i_{2}}\beta_{3}&\beta_{4}&\zeta^{i_{2}}\beta_{5}\\
\gamma_{0}&\zeta^{i_{3}}\gamma_{1}&\gamma_{2}&\zeta^{i_{3}}\gamma_{3}&\gamma_{4}&\zeta^{i_{3}}\gamma_{5}\\
\alpha_{0}&\zeta^{j_{1}}\alpha_{1}&\alpha_{2}&\zeta^{j_{1}}\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{4}&\zeta^{j_{1}}\alpha_{5}\\
\beta_{0}&\zeta^{j_{2}}\beta_{1}&\beta_{2}&\zeta^{j_{2}}\beta_{3}&\beta_{4}&\zeta^{j_{2}}\beta_{5}\\
\gamma_{0}&\zeta^{j_{3}}\gamma_{1}&\gamma_{2}&\zeta^{j_{3}}\gamma_{3}&\gamma_{4}&\zeta^{j_{3}}\gamma_{5}\end{pmatrix}.

It can be easily checked that

(5.2)

\det(A)=c(\zeta^{i_{1}}-\zeta^{j_{1}})(\zeta^{i_{2}}-\zeta^{j_{2}})(\zeta^{i_{3}}-\zeta^{j_{3}})(\alpha_{0}\gamma_{4}\beta_{2}-\alpha_{0}\beta_{4}\gamma_{2}+\beta_{0}\gamma_{2}\alpha_{4}-\beta_{0}\alpha_{2}\gamma_{4}-\gamma_{0}\beta_{2}\alpha_{4}+\gamma_{0}\beta_{4}\alpha_{2}),

where
c:=\det\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1}&\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{5}\\
\beta_{1}&\beta_{3}&\beta_{5}\\
\gamma_{1}&\gamma_{3}&\gamma_{5}\end{pmatrix}.

The calculation (
5.2
) together with the assumption
i_{r}\neq j_{r}
for all
r\in\{1,2,3\},
where
0\leq i_{r},j_{r}\leq n-1
show that the determinant of the matrix
A
is defined by a non-zero polynomial. Thus, there exists a non-empty open subset
U_{(\bar{i},\bar{j})}\subset|\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{5}_{k}}(1)|^{\times 3},
such that for every point
(L^{1},L^{2},L^{3})\in U_{(\bar{i},\bar{j})},
the intersections
L^{1}_{\varphi^{i_{1}}}\cap L^{2}_{\varphi^{i_{2}}}\cap L^{3}_{\varphi^{i_{3}}}
and
L^{1}_{\varphi^{j_{1}}}\cap L^{2}_{\varphi^{j_{2}}}\cap L^{3}_{\varphi^{j_{3}}}
are disjoint, where
\bar{i}:=(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})
and
\bar{j}:=(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3})
. Finally, consider the finite index set
I:=\{(\bar{i},\bar{j})\in\{0,1,2,\ldots,n-1\}^{\times 6}|\ i_{r}\neq j_{r}\}
and put
U:=\bigcap_{(\bar{i},\bar{j})\in I}U_{(\bar{i},\bar{j})}
. Then
U
is a non-empty open subset as
|\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{5}_{k}}(1)|^{\times 3}
is irreducible and clearly the closed points of
U
have the desired property. This proves Lemma
5.3
.
∎

Now, let
H_{i}:=\{F_{i}=0\}\in|\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{5}_{k}}(n)|^{\varphi},
with
i\in\{1,2,3\},
be general invariant hypersurfaces and choose hyperplanes
L^{1},L^{2},L^{3}\in|\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{5}_{k}}(1)|,
as in Lemma
5.3
. We then consider the one parameter family
\mathcal{T}\subset\mathbb{P}^{5}_{k}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
of complete intersections defined by the equations

(5.3)

\begin{split}&(1-t)\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}L^{1}_{\varphi^{i}}+tF_{1}=0,\\
&(1-t)\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}L^{2}_{\varphi^{i}}+tF_{2}=0,\\
&(1-t)\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}L^{3}_{\varphi^{i}}+tF_{3}=0,\end{split}

where
t\in\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
and where by slight abuse of notation we keep the same notation for the equations of
L^{i}_{\varphi^{j}}
. The fibre over
t=0
then consists of
n^{3}
planes
T_{0i}
. The
\mathbb{Z}/n
-orbit of each component
T_{0i}
is a disjoint union of its
n
conjugates and moreover the double intersection
D_{i}:=\bigcup_{j\neq i}(T_{0i}\cap T_{0j})
consists of
3(n-1)
lines that sit inside the plane
T_{0i}
.

Next, we perform the base change
\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k},0}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
and obtain a projective flat family
\mathcal{T}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k},0}
, which is invariant under the
\mathbb{Z}/n
-action and has no fixed points. It is then readily seen that by our construction the family
\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{T}/(\mathbb{Z}/n)\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k},0}
satisfies the properties (
1
), (
2
) and (
3
). The proof of Theorem
5.2
is complete.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

We note that the family
\mathcal{S}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k},0}
constructed in Theorem
5.2
is not semi-stable. For instance there are distinct components in the central fibre whose intersection is a point and those one needs to blow them up further. Eventually the resulting semi-stable degeneration will satisfy the properties (
1
)–(
3
) with the exception in (
2
) all components are rational but not necessarily isomorphic to the projective plane and the double intersections
D_{i}:=\bigcup_{j\neq i}(T_{0i}\cap T_{0j})
will form a cycle of rational curves on each component
T_{0i}
. The case
n=2
yields a Type III semi-stable degeneration of Enriques; see
[
Kul77
, Theorem III]
. The reason we do not use semi-stable families in this paper are the results of
\lx@sectionsign
4
that allow us to work with more general families; see Theorems
4.1
and
4.3
.

6.
Main results

6.1.
Examples with infinite morphic cohomology modulo any prime
\ell

In this subsection, we prove Theorem
1.4
and Corollary
1.5
. These are essential for the proof of Theorem
1.1
, but they are also of independent interest. We begin with the following elementary lemma.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field and let
n\geq 2
be an integer invertible in
k
. Let
X
and
Y
be smooth and equi-dimensional algebraic schemes over
k
and assume that
X
is also proper. Fix a higher Chow cycle
[\Gamma]\in\operatorname{CH}^{c_{1}}(X,p_{1}\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n),
such that its cycle class
\operatorname{cl}_{X}([\Gamma])\in H^{2c_{1}-p_{1}}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n(c_{1}))
is non-zero. Then the kernel of the exterior product map

\operatorname{CH}^{c_{2}}(Y,p_{2}\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\operatorname{CH}^{c_{1}+c_{2}}(X\times Y,p_{1}+p_{2}\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n),[z]\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces[\Gamma\times z]

is contained in
F_{n}^{c_{2},2c_{2}-p_{2}}(Y):=\ker(\operatorname{cl}_{Y}:\operatorname{CH}^{c_{2}}(Y,p_{2}\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\to H^{2c_{2}-p_{2}}(Y_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n(c_{2})))
.

Proof.

Indeed, let
[z]\in\operatorname{CH}^{c_{2}}(Y,p_{2}\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n),
such that
[\Gamma\times z]=0\in\operatorname{CH}^{c_{1}+c_{2}}(X\times Y,p_{1}+p_{2}\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)
. Then we have the equality

0=\operatorname{cl}_{X\times Y}([\Gamma\times z])=p^{\ast}\operatorname{cl}_{X}([\Gamma])\cup q^{\ast}\operatorname{cl}_{Y}([z])\in H^{2(c_{1}+c_{2})-(p_{1}+p_{2})}(X\times Y,\mathbb{Z}/n(c_{1}+c_{2})),

where
p:X\times Y\to X
and
q:X\times Y\to Y
are the natural projections. The class
\operatorname{cl}_{X}([\Gamma])\neq 0
is non-zero and thus, we can consider its Poincaré dual

\overline{\operatorname{cl}_{X}([\Gamma])}\in H^{2(d-c_{1})+p_{1}}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n(d-c_{1})),

i.e.,
\overline{\operatorname{cl}_{X}([\Gamma])}\cup\operatorname{cl}_{X}([\Gamma])=\operatorname{cl}^{d}_{X}(pt)
is the class of a point, where
d:=\dim X
; see
[
Mil80
, Chapter VI, Corollary 11.2]
. We then see that the class
\operatorname{cl}_{Y}([z])
can be recovered as

q_{\ast}(p^{\ast}\operatorname{cl}_{X}^{d}(pt)\cup q^{\ast}\operatorname{cl}_{Y}([z]))=q_{\ast}(p^{\ast}\overline{\operatorname{cl}_{X}([\Gamma])}\cup p^{\ast}\operatorname{cl}_{X}([\Gamma])\cup q^{\ast}\operatorname{cl}_{Y}([z])).

This in turn implies that
\operatorname{cl}_{Y}([z])=0,
as we wanted. The proof of the lemma is complete.
∎

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field and let
k\subset K
be an algebraically closed field extension of positive transcendence degree. Let
E_{K}
be an elliptic curve over
K
whose
J
invariant is transcendental over
k
. Let
n\geq 2
be an integer invertible in
k
. Then for every free
\mathbb{Z}/n
-submodule
M\subset\operatorname{CH}^{1}(E_{K},1\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)
of rank one, the following holds: For any smooth projective variety
Y
over
k
, with base change
Y_{K}:=Y\times_{k}K,
the exterior
product map

(6.1)

M\otimes\operatorname{CH}^{c}(Y,p\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\operatorname{CH}^{c+1}(E_{K}\times Y_{K},p+1\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n),\ [\Gamma]\otimes[z]\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces[\Gamma\times z_{K}]

is injective for all integers
c,p\geq 0
.

Proof.

A rigidity theorem of Jannsen implies that it suffices to consider only the case where the field
K

is an algebraic closure of
k(J)
; see
[
Jan18
, Theorem 0.3]
.

We want to apply Theorem
4.3
. Let
M\subset\operatorname{CH}^{1}(E_{K},1\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)
be a free
\mathbb{Z}/n
-submodule of rank one. Recall that we have a natural isomorphism
\operatorname{CH}^{1}(E_{K},1\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\simeq H^{1}(E_{K},\mathbb{Z}/n)
(see Theorem
2.2
(
1
)) and we let
\tilde{M}\subset H^{1}(E_{K},\mathbb{Z}/n)
be a
\mathbb{Z}/n
-submodule generated by some Poincaré dual of a generator of
M
. Note that
\tilde{M}
as a
\mathbb{Z}/n
-module is also free of rank one and we pick a
\mathbb{Z}/n
-Galois covering
\tilde{E}_{K}\to E_{K}
whose class in
H^{1}(E_{K},\mathbb{Z}/n)
generates
\tilde{M}
; see Remark
4.5
.

We may write
E_{K}\simeq\tilde{E}_{K}/\tau
for some torsion point
\tau\in\tilde{E}_{K}[n]
of order
n
. Let
k(J)\subset F\subset K
be a finite intermediate field extension such that the
n
-torsion points of
\tilde{E}_{K}
are defined over
F
, i.e.,
\tilde{E}_{F}[n]\simeq(\mathbb{Z}/n)^{\oplus 2}
. Let
C
be a smooth projective curve over
k
whose generic point gives the field extension
k\subset F
. We consider the relatively minimal model
p:\mathcal{\tilde{E}}\to C
of the curve
\tilde{E}_{F}
. In fact, we may choose the field
F
above, in such a way that
p:\mathcal{\tilde{E}}\to C
is semi-stable and that every singular fibre has Kodaira type
I_{nm}
for some integer
m\geq 2
; see
[
Sil86
, VII.5.4]
. The identity element
s\in\tilde{E}_{F}
extends to a section
s
of
p
, which we call the identity section. The
n
-torsion point
\tau\in\tilde{E}_{F}[n]
also extends to a section of
p
, which we denote again by the same symbol.

Recall that the set of reduced components of a fiber in a relatively minimal
elliptic model with a section inherits a group structure from the Néron model of
the generic fiber. In the case of a fiber of Kodaira type
I_{N}
this component
group is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/N
. We label the components
R_{i},
with
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,N-1\}
, so that the section
s
meets
R_{0}
and the intersections
R_{i}\cdot R_{i^{\prime}}
are given by

•

R_{i}\cdot R_{i}=-2
,

•

R_{i}\cdot R_{i^{\prime}}=0
if
|i-i^{\prime}|>1
and
|i-i^{\prime}|\neq N-1
,

•

R_{i}\cdot R_{i^{\prime}}=1
if
|i-i^{\prime}|=1
or
|i-i^{\prime}|=N-1
.

Then an isomorphism from the component group to
\mathbb{Z}/N
is given by
R_{i}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces i\in\mathbb{Z}/N
.

We find a point
x_{0}\in C(k)
such that the fibre
p^{-1}(x_{0})
has Kodaira type
I_{nm}
for some
m\geq 2
and such that the unique component of the fibre
p^{-1}(x_{0})
meeting
\tau
has the form
R_{rm}
, with
\gcd(r,n)=1
; see
[
Schoe00
, Lemma 2.7]
. We then observe that the family
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}/\tau\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{C,x_{0}}
is a semi-stable model of the elliptic curve
E_{K}
and has the following properties: Its central fibre has Kodaira type
I_{m},
with
m\geq 2,
and the
\mathbb{Z}/n
-Galois covering
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}\to\tilde{\mathcal{E}}/\tau
is trivial over each component of the central fibre.

We can thus apply Theorem
4.3
for the particular choice of
M
(see Remark
4.4
and Remark
4.5
) and obtain that the exterior product maps

\Lambda:M\otimes\frac{H^{2c-p-1}_{c-p-2,nr}(Y,\mathbb{Z}/n(c))}{H^{2c-p-1}(Y,\mathbb{Z}/n(c))}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces M\otimes\frac{H^{2c-p-1}_{c-p-2,nr}(Y_{K},\mathbb{Z}/n(c))}{H^{2c-p-1}(Y_{K},\mathbb{Z}/n(c))}\overset{\eqref{eq:pairing}}{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\frac{H^{2c-p}_{c-p-2,nr}(E_{K}\times_{K}Y_{K},\mathbb{Z}/n(1+c))}{H^{2c-p}(E_{K}\times_{K}Y_{K},\mathbb{Z}/n(1+c))}

are injective for all
c,p\geq 0
. These maps in turn naturally identify with the exterior product maps

(6.2)

M\otimes F^{c,2c-p}_{n}(Y)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces F^{c+1,2c-p+1}_{n}(E_{K}\times_{K}Y_{K}),[\Gamma]\otimes[z]\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces[\Gamma\times z_{K}]

where
F^{c,2c-p}_{n}(Y):=\ker(\operatorname{cl}_{Y}:\operatorname{CH}^{c}(Y,p\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\to H^{2c-p}(Y_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n(c)))
; see Corollary
3.4
. Finally, we make use of Lemma
6.1
and conclude that the exterior product maps
(
6.1
) are injective for all integers
c,p\geq 0
, since the exterior product maps (
6.2
) are. This proves Theorem
6.2
.
∎

As a consequence of Theorem
6.2
, we obtain the following result.

Corollary 6.3
.

Let
Y
be a smooth complex projective variety. Then there is a smooth complex elliptic curve
E
such that the following holds: For each integer
n\geq 2,
and every free
\mathbb{Z}/n
-submodule
M\subset L^{1}H^{1}(E)/n
of rank one, the exterior product maps

(6.3)

M\otimes L^{c}H^{2c-p}(Y)/n\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces L^{c+1}H^{2c+1-p}(E\times Y)/n,\ \alpha\otimes\beta\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces[\alpha\times\beta]

are injective for all integers
c,p\geq 0
.

Proof.

We may find a countable algebraically closed field
k\subset\mathbb{C}
over which
Y
is defined, i.e.,
Y=Y_{0}\times_{k}\mathbb{C}
. We then consider the Legendre elliptic curve
E_{k(t)}
, which is defined over
k(t),
i.e., the function field of
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}
. The inclusion
k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
factors through
k(t)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
and we can thus apply Theorem
6.2
for
K=\mathbb{C}
. That is, for each
n\geq 2
and every free
\mathbb{Z}/n
-submodule
M\subset\operatorname{CH}^{1}(E,1\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)
of rank one, we find that the exterior product maps

M\otimes\operatorname{CH}^{c}(Y_{0},p\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\overset{\eqref{eq:ext-prod-elliptic}}{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\operatorname{CH}^{c+1}(E\times Y,p+1\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)

are injective for all
c,p\geq 0
, where we set
E:=E_{k(t)}\times_{k(t)}\mathbb{C}
. The rigidity theorem of Jannsen
[
Jan18
, Theorem 0.3]
implies that the base change map
\operatorname{CH}^{c}(Y_{0},p\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\to\operatorname{CH}^{c}(Y,p\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)
is an isomorphism. Moreover, note that we have a natural isomorphism
\operatorname{CH}^{1}(E,1\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\simeq L^{1}H^{1}(E)/n
. This follows from the canonical isomorphism
\operatorname{CH}^{1}(E,1\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\simeq L^{1}H^{1}(E,\mathbb{Z}/n)
(see Theorem
2.8
(
1
)) along with the observation that the canonical injection
L^{1}H^{1}(E)/n\hookrightarrow L^{1}H^{1}(E,\mathbb{Z}/n)
is, in fact, an isomorphism, since
L^{1}H^{2}(E)=\operatorname{CH}^{1}(E)/\sim_{\operatorname{alg}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}
is torsion-free. The comparison theorem between morphic cohomology and higher Chow groups for finite coefficients in turn implies that the exterior product maps

M\otimes\operatorname{CH}^{c}(Y,p\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\overset{\eqref{eq:ext-prod-elliptic}}{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\operatorname{CH}^{c+1}(E\times Y,p+1\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)

identify with the maps

M\otimes L^{c}H^{2c-p}(Y,\mathbb{Z}/n)\overset{\times}{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}L^{c+1}H^{2c+1-p}(E\times Y,\mathbb{Z}/n);

see Theorem
2.8
(
1
). Finally, the injectivity of the latter clearly implies the injectivity of the exterior product maps (
6.3
), as we wanted. The proof of Corollary
6.3
is complete.
∎

We finally see that Theorem
1.4
and Corollary
1.5
are concluded from Corollary
6.3
, as follows.

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

We proceed by induction on
p\geq 0
. If
p=0
, then
L^{2}H^{4}(X)/n\simeq\operatorname{CH}^{2}(X)/n
and one can take, for instance,
X=JC
, as the Jacobian of a very general genus 3 curve
C
; see
[
Tot16
, Corollary 0.1]
. Let
p\geq 1
. The induction hypothesis gives a smooth complex projective variety
Y
of dimension
p+2
such that
L^{p+1}H^{p+3}(Y)/n
contains infinitely many elements of order
n
for all
n\geq 2
. Now, Corollary
6.3
yields a smooth complex elliptic curve
E
such that the following holds: For each
n\geq 2
, and every free
\mathbb{Z}/n
-submodule
M\subset L^{1}H^{1}(E)/n
of rank one, the exterior product map

M\otimes L^{p+1}H^{p+3}(Y)/n\overset{\eqref{eq:ext-prod-elliptic-L}}{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}L^{p+2}H^{p+4}(E\times Y)/n

is injective. Thus, for all
n\geq 2
, the group
L^{p+2}H^{p+4}(E\times Y)/n
contains infinitely many elements of order
n
, as the group
L^{p+1}H^{p+3}(Y)/n
does. The proof of Theorem
1.4
is complete.∎

Proof of Corollary
1.5
.

Let
p\geq 0
and
d\geq p+3
. Then Theorem
1.4
yields a smooth complex projective variety
Y
of dimension
p+3
such that the group
L^{p+2}H^{p+4}(Y)/n
contains infinitely many elements of order
n
for all
n\geq 2
. Then the claim follows from the projective bundle formula applied to the
d
-fold
X:=Y\times\mathbb{P}^{d-(p+3)}_{\mathbb{C}}
; see
[
FG93
, Proposition 2.5]
. The proof of Corollary
1.5
is complete.
∎

Remark 6.4
.

Let
n\geq 2
be an integer. It was crucial for the proof of Theorem
1.4
an example of a smooth complex projective
3
-fold
Y,
such that
\operatorname{CH}^{2}(Y)/n
has infinitely many cycles of order
n
. Instead of Totaro’s example
[
Tot16
]
, we could use any of
[
Dia21
,
Sca24
]
. In particular,
[
Sca24
]
yields examples defined over an algebraic closure of
\mathbb{Q}(t)
.

6.2.
Examples with infinitely many
\ell^{\infty}
-torsion higher Chow cycles modulo a prime
\ell

In this final subsection, we prove Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
together with Corollary
1.2
. Theorem
1.3
will be deduced from the following result.

Theorem 6.5
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field and let
n\geq 2
be an integer invertible in
k
. Let
k\subset K
be an algebraically closed field extension of positive transcendence degree. Consider the degeneration
\mathcal{S}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k},0},
which is constructed in Theorem
5.2
, and let
S_{K}:=\mathcal{S}\times_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k},0}}K
be its geometric generic fibre. Then for every smooth projective variety
Y
over
k
, with base change
Y_{K}:=Y\times_{k}K
, the exterior product map

(6.4)

\operatorname{CH}^{2}(S_{K},1)/n\otimes\operatorname{CH}^{c}(Y,p\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\operatorname{CH}^{c+2}(S_{K}\times_{K}Y_{K},p+1\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n),\ [\Gamma]\otimes[z]\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces[\Gamma\times z_{K}]

is injective for all
c,p\geq 0
.

Proof.

A rigidity theorem of Jannsen implies that it suffices to consider only the case where the field
K
is an algebraic closure of
k(t)
; see
[
Jan18
, Theorem 0.3]
.

We wish to apply Theorem
4.1
. To this end, we need to show that the family
\mathcal{S}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k},0}
satisfies the properties

(
P1
)

and

(
P2
)

. Note that the geometric generic fibre
S_{K}
of this family is an étale
\mathbb{Z}/n
-quotient of some smooth complete intersection
T_{K}
of multidegree
(n,n,n)
within
\mathbb{P}^{5}_{K}
.
The Bockstein map
\delta:H^{1}(S_{K},\mathbb{Z}/n)\to H^{2}_{\operatorname{cont}}(S_{K},\mathbb{Z}_{n})[n]\simeq\mathbb{Z}/n
is an isomorphism, and the generator of the cyclic group
H^{1}(S_{K},\mathbb{Z}/n)\simeq\mathbb{Z}/n
is given by the
\mathbb{Z}/n
-Galois covering
T_{K}\to T_{K}/(\mathbb{Z}/n)=S_{K}
; see Proposition
5.1
(
2
) and Remark
4.5
. This in turn extends to a
\mathbb{Z}/n
-Galois covering
\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{S},
that is trivial over each component
S_{0i}
of the central fibre
S_{0}=\mathcal{S}\times_{{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k},0}}k
; see Theorem
5.2
(
3
). Thus, the class of
\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{S}
in
H^{1}(\mathcal{S}_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n)
satisfies both

(
P1
)

and

(
P2
)

. Note that here once you have

(
P1
)

, then

(
P2
)

is automatic, since all components of the central fibre
S_{0}
are projective planes; see Theorem
5.2
(
2
).

Recall that
\operatorname{CH}^{2}(S_{K},1)/n\simeq\mathbb{Z}/n
is a free
\mathbb{Z}/n
-module of rank one; see Proposition
5.1
(
3
). It follows from Theorem
4.1
that for
M=\operatorname{CH}^{2}(S_{K},1)/n
and any smooth projective variety
Y
over
k
, with base change
Y_{K}=Y\times_{k}K,

the exterior product maps

\Lambda:M\otimes\frac{H^{2c-p-1}_{c-p-2,nr}(Y,\mathbb{Z}/n(c))}{H^{2c-p-1}(Y,\mathbb{Z}/n(c))}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces M\otimes\frac{H^{2c-p-1}_{c-p-2,nr}(Y_{K},\mathbb{Z}/n(c))}{H^{2c-p-1}(Y_{K},\mathbb{Z}/n(c))}\overset{\eqref{eq:pairing}}{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\frac{H^{2+2c-p}_{c-p-1,nr}(S_{K}\times_{K}Y_{K},\mathbb{Z}/n(2+c))}{H^{2+2c-p}(S_{K}\times_{K}Y_{K},\mathbb{Z}/n(2+c))}

are injective for all
c,p\geq 0
. These maps in turn naturally identify with the exterior product maps

(6.5)

\operatorname{CH}^{2}(S_{K},1)/n\otimes F^{c,2c-p}_{n}(Y)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces F^{c+2,2c-p+3}_{n}(S_{K}\times_{K}Y_{K}),[\Gamma]\otimes[z]\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces[\Gamma\times z_{K}]

where
F^{c,2c-p}_{n}(Y):=\ker(\operatorname{cl}_{Y}:\operatorname{CH}^{c}(Y,p\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\to H^{2c-p}(Y_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n(c)))
; see Corollary
3.4
. Finally, the injectivity of the cycle class map
\operatorname{cl}_{S_{K}}:\operatorname{CH}^{2}(S_{K},1)/n\hookrightarrow H^{3}({S_{K}}_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n(2))
(see Lemma
2.4
(
1
)) together with the injectivity of the exterior product maps (
6.5
) imply that the maps

\operatorname{CH}^{2}(S_{K},1)/n\otimes\operatorname{CH}^{c}(Y,p\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\overset{\eqref{eq:ext-prod-S-Z/n}}{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\operatorname{CH}^{c+2}(S_{K}\times_{K}Y_{K},p+1\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)

are also injective for all
c,p\geq 0
; see Lemma
6.1
. This concludes the proof of Theorem
6.5
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Let
n\geq 2
be an integer and let
Y
be a smooth complex projective variety. Then we can find a countable algebraically closed field
k\subset\mathbb{C}
such that
Y
is defined over
k,
i.e.,
Y=Y_{0}\times_{k}\mathbb{C}
. Consider the degeneration
\mathcal{S}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k},0}
constructed in Theorem
5.2
. Since
k
is a countable field, the extension
k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
factors through
k\subset k(t)
. Thus, Theorem
6.5
for
K=\mathbb{C}
implies that the exterior product map

(6.6)

\operatorname{CH}^{2}(S,1)/n\otimes\operatorname{CH}^{c}(Y,p\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\operatorname{CH}^{c+2}(S\times_{\mathbb{C}}Y,p+1\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)

is injective for all
c,p\geq 0
, where we set
S:=\mathcal{S}\times_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k},0}}\mathbb{C}
. It is important to note that above we have implicitly used that the base change map
\operatorname{CH}^{c}(Y_{0},p\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)\to\operatorname{CH}^{c}(Y,p\ ;\mathbb{Z}/n)
is an isomorphism; see
[
Jan18
, Theorem 0.3]
. Recall that the surface
S
satisfies
\operatorname{Pic}(S)_{\operatorname{tors}}=\operatorname{NS}(S)_{\operatorname{tors}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}/n
and thus we obtain a canonical isomorphism
\operatorname{CH}^{2}(S,1)/n\simeq L^{2}H^{3}(S)/n\simeq\mathbb{Z}/n
; see Lemma
2.9
(
2
). Moreover, we find that the exterior product map (
6.6
) identifies with

(6.7)

L^{2}H^{3}(S)/n\otimes L^{c}H^{2c-p}(Y,\mathbb{Z}/n)\overset{\times}{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}L^{c+2}H^{2c-p+3}(S\times Y,\mathbb{Z}/n)

for all
c,p\geq 0
; see Theorem
2.8
(
1
). Finally, note that we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L^{2}H^{3}(S)/n\otimes L^{c}H^{2c-p}(Y,\mathbb{Z}/n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L^{c+2}H^{2c-p+3}(S\times Y,\mathbb{Z}/n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}H^{3}(S)/n\otimes L^{c}H^{2c-p}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{c+2}H^{2c-p+3}(S\times Y)/n,}
(
6.7
)
(
1.3
)

where the vertical maps are injective. Hence, we see that the exterior product map (
1.3
) is injective, since the exterior product map (
6.7
) is. The proof of Theorem
1.3
is complete.∎

The following is the analogue of Theorem
1.1
for morphic cohomology.

Theorem 6.6
.

For each
p\geq 1
and
n\geq 2
, there is a smooth complex projective variety
X
of dimension
p+4
such that the group
L^{p+3}H^{p+6}(X)[n]/nL^{p+3}H^{p+6}(X)[n^{2}]
contains infinitely many elements of order
n
.

Proof.

Let
p\geq 1
and
n\geq 2
. Then Theorem
1.4
yields a smooth complex projective variety
Y
of dimension
p+2
such that the group
L^{p+1}H^{p+3}(Y)/n
contains infinitely many elements of order
n
for all integers
n\geq 2
. On the other hand, we can find a smooth complex projective surface
S
with
\operatorname{Pic}(S)_{\operatorname{tors}}=\operatorname{NS}(S)_{\operatorname{tors}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}/n
such that the exterior product map

L^{2}H^{3}(S)/n\otimes L^{p+1}H^{p+3}(Y)/n\overset{\eqref{eq:exterior-product}}{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}L^{p+3}H^{p+6}(S\times Y)/n

is injective; see Theorem
1.3
. Recall that we have an equality
L^{2}H^{3}(S)[n]/nL^{2}H^{3}(S)[n^{2}]=L^{2}H^{3}(S)/n
and that the latter group is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/n
; see Lemma
2.9
(
2
). All together imply that we have an injection

L^{p+1}H^{p+3}(Y)/n\simeq L^{2}H^{3}(S)/n\otimes L^{p+1}H^{p+3}(Y)/n\overset{\eqref{eq:exterior-product}}{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}L^{p+3}H^{p+6}(X)[n]/nL^{p+3}H^{p+6}(X)[n^{2}],

where we set
X:=S\times Y
. Hence, the group
L^{p+3}H^{p+6}(X)[n]/nL^{p+3}H^{p+6}(X)[n^{2}]
has infinitely many elements of order
n
, as the group
L^{p+1}H^{p+3}(Y)/n
also has. The proof of Theorem
6.6
is complete.
∎

We can finally deduce Theorem
1.1
and Corollary
1.2
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Let
p\geq 1
and
n\geq 2
. Then Theorem
6.6
gives a smooth complex projective variety
X
of dimension
p+4
such that the group
L^{p+3}H^{p+6}(X)[n]/nL^{p+3}H^{p+6}(X)[n^{2}]
has infinitely many elements of order
n
. Thus, the claim follows from the canonical isomorphism

\operatorname{CH}^{p+3}(X,p)[n]/n\operatorname{CH}^{p+3}(X,p)[n^{2}]\simeq L^{p+3}H^{p+6}(X)[n]/nL^{p+3}H^{p+6}(X)[n^{2}];

see Theorem
2.8
(
2
). The proof of Theorem
1.1
is complete.∎

Proof of Corollary
1.2
.

Let
p\geq 1,d\geq p+4
and
n\geq 2
. Then Theorem
1.1
yields a smooth complex projective variety
Y
of dimension
p+4
such that the group
\operatorname{CH}^{p+3}(X,p)[n]/n\operatorname{CH}^{p+3}(X,p)[n^{2}]
contains infinitely many cycles of order
n
. Thus, the result follows from the projective bundle formula applied to the
d
-fold
X:=Y\times\mathbb{P}^{d-(p+4)}_{\mathbb{C}}
; see
[
Blo86
, Theorem 7.1]
. The proof of Corollary
1.2
is complete.∎

Remark 6.7
.

Let
n\geq 2
. Recall that for
p=1
, the smooth complex projective
5
-fold
X
of Theorem
1.1
is given by the product
X:=S\times JC
of the surface
S
from Theorem
1.3
, with the Jacobian
JC
of a very general quartic plane curve
C\subset\mathbb{P}^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}
. In this case the infinitely many cycles considered in
\operatorname{CH}^{4}(X,1)[n]
all lie in the kernel of the natural map
\operatorname{cl}_{X}:\operatorname{CH}^{4}(X,1)\to H^{7}_{L}(X,\mathbb{Z}(4))
.

Indeed, the infinitely many cycles in
\operatorname{CH}^{2}(JC)/n\simeq L^{2}H^{4}(X)/n
arise from pulling back the Ceresa cycle
[
Cer83
]
by one of infinitely many distinct isogenies; see
[
Tot16
]
. Hence, they are all nullhomologous and in particular lie in the kernel
E^{2}_{n}(JC):=\ker(\bar{\operatorname{cl}}_{JC}:\operatorname{CH}^{2}(JC)/n\to H^{4}(JC_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n(2)))
. Now, for
[\Gamma]\in\operatorname{CH}^{2}(S,1)[n]
and
[z]\in E^{2}_{n}(JC)
, one finds that the class
\operatorname{cl}_{S\times JC}([\Gamma\times z])=p^{\ast}\operatorname{cl}_{S}([\Gamma])\cup q^{\ast}\operatorname{cl}_{JC}([z])=0
is zero, where
p:S\times JC\to S
and
q:S\times JC\to JC
are the natural projections. This is because
\operatorname{cl}_{S}([\Gamma])
is
n
-torsion, while
\operatorname{cl}_{JC}([z])
is divisible by
n
.

Remark 6.8
.

Let
p\geq 1
and
n\geq 2
. Let
X
be as in the proof of Theorem
1.1
(or Theorem
6.6
). As in the case
p=1
(see Remark
6.7
), one sees that the kernel of the cycle class map
\operatorname{cl}^{L}_{X}:\operatorname{CH}^{p+3}(X,p)[n]\to H^{p+6}_{L}(X,\mathbb{Z}(p))[n]
is infinite, since the
n
-torsion subgroup of
H^{p+6}_{L}(X,\mathbb{Z}(p))
is finite. However, if
p\geq 2
, then our
n
-torsion cycles in
\operatorname{CH}^{p+3}(X,p)
are no longer chosen canonically, that is, these are lifts of
n
-torsion classes in
L^{p+3}H^{p+6}(X)
. This in turn prevents us from computing their value via the above cycle map
\operatorname{cl}^{L}_{X}
. Instead, if one considers the cycle map
\operatorname{cl}^{B}_{X}:\operatorname{CH}^{p+3}(X,p)\to H^{p+6}_{B}(X,\mathbb{Z}(p))
, with values in Betti cohomology, then we find that the infinitely many
n
-torsion cycles in
\operatorname{CH}^{p+3}(X,p)
all lie in the kernel of
\operatorname{cl}^{B}_{X}
.

Indeed, the latter cycle class map factors through the
s
-map
L^{p+3}H^{p+6}(X)\to H^{p+6}_{B}(X,\mathbb{Z})
constructed in
[
FM94
]
. Recall that
X
is given by the product
S\times Y
, where
S
is the surface of Theorem
1.3
and where
Y:=E_{1}\times\cdots\times E_{p-1}\times JC
is the product of
p-1
very general elliptic curves
E_{i}
with the Jacobian
JC
of a very general genus
3
curve
C
. By our construction the infinitely many
n
-torsion cycles in
L^{p+3}H^{p+6}(X)
are given as exterior products
[\Gamma\times z]
, where
[\Gamma]\in L^{2}H^{3}(S)/n
and
[z]\in\ker(L^{p+1}H^{p+3}(Y)/n\to H^{p+3}(Y_{\text{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/n(p+1)))
; see proofs of Theorems
6.6
and
1.4
. Thus, same reasoning as in Remark
6.7
, explains that the
n
-torsion classes considered in
L^{p+3}H^{p+6}(X)
all lie in the kernel of the
s
-map.
